People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, thanks for having me. I guess what brings me in is... well, since I stopped working, I just can’t seem to slow down. I’m up and moving from one little job to the next all day long, just like I still have a crew waiting on me. 

I used to run a crew, you know. Good group of guys, mostly. Though we had this one kid, Mikey, who managed to back the work truck into a portable toilet once. Nobody was in it, thank God, but man, what a mess. We had to hose down the whole lot. Which actually reminds me, I need to buy a new hose for the front yard. The rubber on the old one completely cracked over the winter. You really can't leave those things out in the freeze, the ice just tears them up from the inside. I tried fixing it with duct tape, but that never really holds water pressure, does it? 

Anyway, wait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, why I'm here. Yeah, I just can't seem to stop moving around the house.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, nothing like that. My mood's been fine, honestly. Just normal, I guess. I mean, I don't feel on top of the world or anything, and I'm not snapping at people. Well, except maybe the guy at the hardware store last week, but that wasn't my mood, that was just him being completely useless. I went in looking for a specific type of galvanized nail for this birdhouse I'm building. You know, the ones that don't rust? Because if you use regular nails on outdoor stuff, they bleed rust down the wood after the first rain, and it just looks terrible. Anyway, I asked him where they were, and he pointed me to the plumbing aisle! Can you believe that? Plumbing! I spent twenty minutes looking at PVC pipes before I realized. I should have just gone to the local place down the street, they always know where everything is. The owner there, Bob, he's been running it since the eighties. Good guy. 

But yeah, no, my mood is regular. Just... regular me.

Well, you know, it really became a big problem after I retired from doing handyman work. That was about a year and two months ago. I figured I'd finally have time to sit on the porch and just relax, you know? My wife bought me this really nice rocking chair for my retirement. Solid oak, beautiful craftsmanship. The guy who makes them lives a couple of towns over, and he hand-carves the armrests. He actually uses this special oil finish that brings out the grain perfectly. I tried doing that once on a coffee table I built, but I didn't let the wood dry out enough first, so the finish got all cloudy. You really have to be patient with woodworking, let the wood acclimate to the humidity in the room. If it's too damp, it swells, and then when it dries, your joints get loose. I hate loose joints on furniture. It just feels cheap, you know? Like those flat-pack things you buy at the big box stores. You put them together with a little hex wrench and a week later they're wobbling every time you set a cup down.

Solid oak, beautiful craftsmanship. The guy who makes them lives a couple of towns over, and he hand-carves the armrests. He actually uses this special oil finish that brings out the grain perfectly. I tried doing that once on a coffee table I built, but I didn't let the wood dry out enough first, so the finish got all cloudy. You really have to be patient with woodworking, let the wood acclimate to the humidity in the room. If it's too damp, it swells, and then when it dries, your joints get loose. I hate loose joints on furniture. It just feels cheap, you know? Like those flat-pack things you buy at the big box stores. You put them together with a little hex wrench and a week later they're wobbling every time you set a cup down. \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, the restlessness and all that, it really kicked into high gear after I stopped working a little over a year ago.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my wife definitely notices. She's always having to remind me about paying a bill, or else I just completely forget. And lately, I've been dropping the ball on other stuff too, like... oh, returning calls from the community center. They've been trying to get me to come down and help with the garden group. I promised I'd bring some supplies for them, but I keep forgetting to actually pack them up. 

Speaking of the community center, they just put in these new raised beds for the seniors. Really nice cedar wood. Cedar is great for that kind of thing because it naturally resists rot and insects. You don't even have to treat it, which is perfect if you're growing vegetables. You don't want chemicals leaching into your tomatoes, right? My neighbor, Frank, he used pressure-treated lumber for his garden boxes a few years back, and I told him, "Frank, you're gonna end up eating whatever they soaked that wood in." He didn't listen, of course. Frank never listens. He's the same guy who tried to rewire his own garage and ended up blowing the transformer for our whole block. We were without power for two days in the middle of July. Had to throw out everything in the freezer. My wife was furious because she had just made a huge batch of her famous chili and froze half of it. She uses three different kinds of beans, you know. 

But yeah, the forgetting things... it's definitely been happening more. The bills, the calls, the garden stuff. It just slips my mind.

You really have to be patient with woodworking, let the wood acclimate to the humidity in the room. If it's too damp, it swells, and then when it dries, your joints get loose. I hate loose joints on furniture. It just feels cheap, you know? Like those flat-pack things you buy at the big box stores. Yeah, the restlessness and all that, it really kicked into high gear after I stopped working a little over a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone close to you noticed that you're having more trouble than before with things like remembering appointments, following conversations, or keeping track of what you were doing?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife definitely notices. She's always having to remind me about paying a bill, or else I just completely forget. And lately, I've been dropping the ball on other stuff too, like... oh, returning calls from the community center. They've been trying to get me to come down and help with the garden group. I promised I'd bring some supplies for them, but I keep forgetting to actually pack them up. \n\nSpeaking of the community center, they just put in these new raised beds for the seniors. Really nice cedar wood. Cedar is great for that kind of thing because it naturally resists rot and insects. You don't even have to treat it, which is perfect if you're growing vegetables. You don't want chemicals leaching into your tomatoes, right? My neighbor, Frank, he used pressure-treated lumber for his garden boxes a few years back, and I told him, \"Frank, you're gonna end up eating whatever they soaked that wood in.\" He didn't listen, of course. Frank never listens. He's the same guy who tried to rewire his own garage and ended up blowing the transformer for our whole block. We were without power for two days in the middle of July. Had to throw out everything in the freezer. My wife was furious because she had just made a huge batch of her famous chili and froze half of it. No, I don't really get anxious like that. My health is fine, and we get by okay with the retirement money. I'm not one to sit around chewing my fingernails over things. 

My brother-in-law Gary, though, he is an absolute nervous wreck about his finances and the economy. He actually started buying those emergency survival food buckets they advertise on TV late at night. You know the ones I mean? They come in these massive plastic tubs and they claim the food lasts for twenty-five years. I told him Gary, if things get that bad, nobody is going to want to sit around eating freeze-dried beef stroganoff. I actually looked at the ingredients on one of his packets once, and it is just packed with sodium and artificial junk. You would honestly be way better off just learning how to can your own vegetables. 

My grandmother used to can everything when I was a kid. We had a whole root cellar just lined with mason jars of pickled beets and green beans. You had to use those thick glass jars with the rubber gaskets, and you had to boil them in a water bath just right to get the lid to seal. If you messed up the seal, the food would spoil and you could get botulism, which is a pretty terrible way to go. You have to be real careful with pressure canners, too. I had a buddy whose canner blew a safety valve once and it shot a dent right into his kitchen ceiling. Plaster everywhere. 

But anyway... wait, did you ask about my health? No, worrying. Right. Yeah, no, I don't really worry about that kind of stuff. I just take things one day at a time.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... yeah, it's not really new. Even back in grade school, I remember my teachers always telling my parents I was bright but just scattered. I was always losing my pencils, calling out answers before they even finished the question, that kind of thing. They were always having to remind me to finish my work before recess. 

Recess was the best part of the day, honestly. We had this huge wooden playground structure—one of those old-school ones they don't build anymore because they're afraid kids will get a splinter or fall off. It had these big tire swings, and we'd try to spin them as fast as we could until we were dizzy. Kids these days don't get to play on stuff like that. Everything is plastic and covered in that bouncy rubber mulch. My grandson's school has that rubber stuff, and let me tell you, when the sun hits it in the middle of the afternoon, it smells exactly like a tire fire. I don't know how the kids can even breathe out there. Plus, I read an article that said those recycled tires can leach chemicals into the ground. It just doesn't seem right. Give me good old-fashioned wood chips or sand any day. Sand is great, except when the neighborhood cats decide to use it as a litter box. You really have to cover a sandbox if you build one in your backyard. I built one for my kids back in the eighties, used some nice pressure-treated 4x4s...

Oh, but yeah, the school stuff. Yeah, the teachers were always on my case about being scattered and losing things, calling out. So I guess it's been there a long time.

Because if you use regular nails on outdoor stuff, they bleed rust down the wood after the first rain, and it just looks terrible. Anyway, I asked him where they were, and he pointed me to the plumbing aisle! Can you believe that? Plumbing! I spent twenty minutes looking at PVC pipes before I realized. I should have just gone to the local place down the street, they always know where everything is. The owner there, Bob, he's been running it since the eighties. Good guy. \n\nBut yeah, no, my mood is regular. Just... regular me.\n\nDoctor: Before you stopped working, did you notice this same kind of restlessness and trouble focusing, or is this something that started only after you retired?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it really became a big problem after I retired from doing handyman work. That was about a year and two months ago. I figured I'd finally have time to sit on the porch and just relax, you know? My wife bought me this really nice rocking chair for my retirement. Solid oak, beautiful craftsmanship. The guy who makes them lives a couple of towns over, and he hand-carves the armrests. He actually uses this special oil finish that brings out the grain perfectly. I tried doing that once on a coffee table I built, but I didn't let the wood dry out enough first, so the finish got all cloudy. You really have to be patient with woodworking, let the wood acclimate to the humidity in the room. If it's too damp, it swells, and then when it dries, your joints get loose. I hate loose joints on furniture. It just feels cheap, you know? Like those flat-pack things you buy at the big box stores. You put them together with a little hex wrench and a week later they're wobbling every time you set a cup down. \n\nWait, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yeah, the restlessness and all that, it really kicked into high gear after I stopped working a little over a year ago.\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone close to you noticed that you're having more trouble than before with things like remembering appointments, following conversations, or keeping track of what you were doing?\n\nPatient: Well, my wife definitely notices. She's always having to remind me about paying a bill, or else I just completely forget. And lately, I've been dropping the ball on other stuff too, like... oh, returning calls from the community center. They've been trying to get me to come down and help with the garden group. I promised I'd bring some supplies for them, but I keep forgetting to actually pack them up. \n\nSpeaking of the community center, they just put in these new raised beds for the seniors. Really nice cedar wood. Cedar is great for that kind of thing because it naturally resists rot and insects. You don't even have to treat it, which is perfect if you're growing vegetables. You don't want chemicals leaching into your tomatoes, right? My neighbor, Frank, he used pressure-treated lumber for his garden boxes a few years back, and I told him, \"Frank, you're gonna end up eating whatever they soaked that wood in.\" He didn't listen, of course. Frank never listens. He's the same guy who tried to rewire his own garage and ended up blowing the transformer for our whole block. We were without power for two days in the middle of July. Had to throw out everything in the freezer. My wife was furious because she had just made a huge batch of her famous chili and froze half of it. She uses three different kinds of beans, you know. \n\nBut yeah, the forgetting things... it's definitely been happening more. The bills, the calls, the garden stuff. It just slips my mind.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, do you find yourself worrying a lot, or feeling anxious about things like your health, finances, or daily tasks, even when there's no clear reason to?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get anxious like that. My health is fine, and we get by okay with the retirement money. I'm not one to sit around chewing my fingernails over things. \n\nMy brother-in-law Gary, though, he is an absolute nervous wreck about his finances and the economy. He actually started buying those emergency survival food buckets they advertise on TV late at night. You know the ones I mean? They come in these massive plastic tubs and they claim the food lasts for twenty-five years. I told him Gary, if things get that bad, nobody is going to want to sit around eating freeze-dried beef stroganoff. I actually looked at the ingredients on one of his packets once, and it is just packed with sodium and artificial junk. You would honestly be way better off just learning how to can your own vegetables. \n\nMy grandmother used to can everything when I was a kid. We had a whole root cellar just lined with mason jars of pickled beets and green beans. You had to use those thick glass jars with the rubber gaskets, and you had to boil them in a water bath just right to get the lid to seal. If you messed up the seal, the food would spoil and you could get botulism, which is a pretty terrible way to go. You have to be real careful with pressure canners, too. I had a buddy whose canner blew a safety valve once and it shot a dent right into his kitchen ceiling. Plaster everywhere. \n\nBut anyway... wait, did you ask about my health? No, worrying. Right. Yeah, no, I don't really worry about that kind of stuff. I just take things one day at a time.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were younger—say, in school or your early working years—did you ever struggle with restlessness, staying focused, or forgetfulness like this before, or is this really the first time in your life you've experienced it?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... yeah, it's not really new. Even back in grade school, I remember my teachers always telling my parents I was bright but just scattered. I was always losing my pencils, calling out answers before they even finished the question, that kind of thing. They were always having to remind me to finish my work before recess. \n\nRecess was the best part of the day, honestly. We had this huge wooden playground structure—one of those old-school ones they don't build anymore because they're afraid kids will get a splinter or fall off. It had these big tire swings, and we'd try to spin them as fast as we could until we were dizzy. Kids these days don't get to play on stuff like that. Everything is plastic and covered in that bouncy rubber mulch. My grandson's school has that rubber stuff, and let me tell you, when the sun hits it in the middle of the afternoon, it smells exactly like a tire fire. I don't know how the kids can even breathe out there. Plus, I read an article that said those recycled tires can leach chemicals into the ground. It just doesn't seem right. Give me good old-fashioned wood chips or sand any day. Sand is great, except when the neighborhood cats decide to use it as a litter box. You really have to cover a sandbox if you build one in your backyard. I built one for my kids back in the eighties, used some nice pressure-treated 4x4s...\n\nOh, but yeah, the school stuff. Yeah, the teachers were always on my case about being scattered and losing things, calling out. So I guess it's been there a long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Mild cognitive changes (F06.7) remain a differential given memory lapses, but the longstanding developmental pattern favors ADHD.", "is_final": false}

Really nice cedar wood. Cedar is great for that kind of thing because it naturally resists rot and insects. You don't even have to treat it, which is perfect if you're growing vegetables. My neighbor, Frank, he used pressure-treated lumber for his garden boxes a few years back, and I told him, \"Frank, you're gonna end up eating whatever they soaked that wood in.\" He didn't listen, of course. Frank never listens. He's the same guy who tried to rewire his own garage and ended up blowing the transformer for our whole block. We were without power for two days in the middle of July. Had to throw out everything in the freezer. My wife was furious because she had just made a huge batch of her famous chili and froze half of it. She uses three different kinds of beans, you know. \n\nBut yeah, the forgetting things... it's definitely been happening more. The bills, the calls, the garden stuff. It just slips my mind.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the restlessness, do you find yourself worrying a lot, or feeling anxious about things like your health, finances, or daily tasks, even when there's no clear reason to?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really get anxious like that. My health is fine, and we get by okay with the retirement money. I'm not one to sit around chewing my fingernails over things. \n\nMy brother-in-law Gary, though, he is an absolute nervous wreck about his finances and the economy. He actually started buying those emergency survival food buckets they advertise on TV late at night. You know the ones I mean? They come in these massive plastic tubs and they claim the food lasts for twenty-five years. I told him Gary, if things get that bad, nobody is going to want to sit around eating freeze-dried beef stroganoff. I actually looked at the ingredients on one of his packets once, and it is just packed with sodium and artificial junk. You would honestly be way better off just learning how to can your own vegetables. \n\nMy grandmother used to can everything when I was a kid. We had a whole root cellar just lined with mason jars of pickled beets and green beans. You had to use those thick glass jars with the rubber gaskets, and you had to boil them in a water bath just right to get the lid to seal. If you messed up the seal, the food would spoil and you could get botulism, which is a pretty terrible way to go. You have to be real careful with pressure canners, too. I had a buddy whose canner blew a safety valve once and it shot a dent right into his kitchen ceiling. Plaster everywhere. \n\nBut anyway... wait, did you ask about my health? No, worrying. Right. Yeah, no, I don't really worry about that kind of stuff. I just take things one day at a time.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back to when you were younger—say, in school or your early working years—did you ever struggle with restlessness, staying focused, or forgetfulness like this before, or is this really the first time in your life you've experienced it?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... yeah, it's not really new. Even back in grade school, I remember my teachers always telling my parents I was bright but just scattered. I was always losing my pencils, calling out answers before they even finished the question, that kind of thing. They were always having to remind me to finish my work before recess. \n\nRecess was the best part of the day, honestly. We had this huge wooden playground structure—one of those old-school ones they don't build anymore because they're afraid kids will get a splinter or fall off. It had these big tire swings, and we'd try to spin them as fast as we could until we were dizzy. Kids these days don't get to play on stuff like that. Everything is plastic and covered in that bouncy rubber mulch. My grandson's school has that rubber stuff, and let me tell you, when the sun hits it in the middle of the afternoon, it smells exactly like a tire fire. I don't know how the kids can even breathe out there. Plus, I read an article that said those recycled tires can leach chemicals into the ground. It just doesn't seem right. Give me good old-fashioned wood chips or sand any day. Sand is great, except when the neighborhood cats decide to use it as a litter box. You really have to cover a sandbox if you build one in your backyard. I built one for my kids back in the eighties, used some nice pressure-treated 4x4s...\n\nOh, but yeah, the school stuff. Yeah, the teachers were always on my case about being scattered and losing things, calling out. So I guess it's been there a long time.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your wife or anyone else told you that the way you talk—going off on these long tangents and stories—is new for you, or have you always been someone who talks this way, even when you were younger?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, my wife would tell you I've always been a talker. She says I could talk the ear off a brass monkey. So no, the stories and all that, that's not new at all. I've been telling stories since I was a kid. 

My dad was a big storyteller, too. He used to sit on the front porch with his buddies for hours, just swapping tales about the old days. They’d smoke these cheap cigars—Swisher Sweets, I think they were called. You could smell them from three houses down. Honestly, I don't know how my mother put up with it. She hated the smell of cigar smoke. She used to make him take his jacket off and leave it in the garage before he came in the house. The garage always smelled like a mix of old tobacco and motor oil. Dad was always tinkering with his car out there. He had this beautiful 1968 Chevy Impala. Cherry red. He wouldn't let anyone else drive it, not even my older brother when he got his license. My brother was so mad, he went out and bought a beat-up Ford Pinto just to spite him. Worst car ever made, those Pintos. You know they had that design flaw where the gas tank was right in the back, and if you got rear-ended, the whole thing could just burst into flames? I saw a documentary about it once on public television. It's crazy what car companies used to get away with before they had all these safety regulations...

But anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, she'll tell you I've always talked like this. It's just how I am.

She says I could talk the ear off a brass monkey. So no, the stories and all that, that's not new at all. I've been telling stories since I was a kid. \n\nMy dad was a big storyteller, too. He used to sit on the front porch with his buddies for hours, just swapping tales about the old days. They’d smoke these cheap cigars—Swisher Sweets, I think they were called. You could smell them from three houses down. Honestly, I don't know how my mother put up with it. She hated the smell of cigar smoke. She used to make him take his jacket off and leave it in the garage before he came in the house. The garage always smelled like a mix of old tobacco and motor oil. Dad was always tinkering with his car out there. He had this beautiful 1968 Chevy Impala. Cherry red. He wouldn't let anyone else drive it, not even my older brother when he got his license. My brother was so mad, he went out and bought a beat-up Ford Pinto just to spite him. Worst car ever made, those Pintos. You know they had that design flaw where the gas tank was right in the back, and if you got rear-ended, the whole thing could just burst into flames? I saw a documentary about it once on public television. It's crazy what car companies used to get away with before they had all these safety regulations...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about? Oh, right, my wife. Yeah, she'll tell you I've always talked like this. It's just how I am."
  }
]

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected. Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, you know, it's funny you ask that, because... yeah, it's not really new. I was always losing my pencils, calling out answers before they even finished the question, that kind of thing. They were always having to remind me to finish my work before recess. \n\nRecess was the best part of the day, honestly. We had this huge wooden playground structure—one of those old-school ones they don't build anymore because they're afraid kids will get a splinter or fall off. It had these big tire swings, and we'd try to spin them as fast as we could until we were dizzy. Kids these days don't get to play on stuff like that. Everything is plastic and covered in that bouncy rubber mulch. My grandson's school has that rubber stuff, and let me tell you, when the sun hits it in the middle of the afternoon, it smells exactly like a tire fire. I don't know how the kids can even breathe out there. Plus, I read an article that said those recycled tires can leach chemicals into the ground. It just doesn't seem right. Give me good old-fashioned wood chips or sand any day. Sand is great, except when the neighborhood cats decide to use it as a litter box. You really have to cover a sandbox if you build one in your backyard. I built one for my kids back in the eighties, used some nice pressure-treated 4x4s...\n\nOh, but yeah, the school stuff. Yeah, the teachers were always on my case about being scattered and losing things, calling out. So I guess it's been there a long time.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWell, my wife would tell you I've always been a talker. She says I could talk the ear off a brass monkey. He used to sit on the front porch with his buddies for hours, just swapping tales about the old days. They’d smoke these cheap cigars—Swisher Sweets, I think they were called. You could smell them from three houses down. Honestly, I don't know how my mother put up with it. She hated the smell of cigar smoke. She used to make him take his jacket off and leave it in the garage before he came in the house. The garage always smelled like a mix of old tobacco and motor oil. Dad was always tinkering with his car out there. He had this beautiful 1968 Chevy Impala. Cherry red. He wouldn't let anyone else drive it, not even my older brother when he got his license. My brother was so mad, he went out and bought a beat-up Ford Pinto just to spite him. Worst car ever made, those Pintos. You know they had that design flaw where the gas tank was right in the back, and if you got rear-ended, the whole thing could just burst into flames? I saw a documentary about it once on public television. It's crazy what car companies used to get away with before they had all these safety regulations...\n\nBut anyway, what were we talking about?
